**Tests/verification:** `pnpm exec tsc --noEmit`, `pnpm exec eslint .`, `pnpm vitest run` (430 tests, 44 files, all passing), `pnpm run build` all clean. Committed `e869897`, pushed to `main`, deployed to production (aliased `lifeos-seven-rho.vercel.app`).

**Live-verified (production):** signed in as Richard, clicked Refresh brief before the email backfill/deploy — confirmed via direct read of `briefs.delivered_channels` that it recorded `["in_app"]` only (email attempted, silently failed with "recipient has no email on file" per the channel's own code path). Applied the code fix, backfilled the existing row, redeployed, clicked Refresh brief again — `briefs.delivered_channels` now recorded `["in_app","email"]`, confirming Resend actually accepted and sent the message to `rwsmith964@gmail.com`. No destructive action taken; the backfill only filled a null field with the account's own already-known login email.

## D-092 | 2026-08-31 | Your own work schedule / routine has nowhere to be entered

**Problem (user feedback):** "my profile, as the user, has basically no information about me calendar wise. For example I cannot include my work schedule or anything and I need to be able to build out my normal routine and schedule in it." Investigation confirmed the underlying feature (recurring weekly `work_schedules` + dated `time_off_entries`, D-064) was already fully built and already renders on `/calendar` and feeds the weekend planner's free/busy logic (D-068) — but only for other household members, reachable via each person's `/people/[id]` detail page. The account owner's own "self" person record is deliberately excluded from the `/people` list (P0-5: "self is who's using the app, not someone they're keeping track of") — which meant there was no reachable page in the entire app for the owner to enter their own routine, even though the data model and scoring already fully supported it.

**Fix (reuse, not rebuild):** added a "Your work schedule" and "Your time off" section to Settings (`app/(app)/settings/my-schedule.tsx`), rendering the exact same list/add/delete markup as the person detail page and reusing its existing components (`AddWorkScheduleForm`, `DeleteWorkScheduleButton`, `AddTimeOffForm`, `DeleteTimeOffButton`) and server actions (`addWorkScheduleAction`, etc.) unmodified — no parallel schedule logic, single source of truth stays in `lib/db/repositories/work-schedule.ts`. `app/(app)/settings/page.tsx` now looks up the household's `self` person record and fetches their schedule rows the same way the person page does. Extended the four work-schedule/time-off actions' existing `revalidatePath` calls to also revalidate `/settings`, since it's now a second page displaying this same data (mirrors how they already revalidate both `/people/[id]` and `/calendar`).

**Not needed:** no new table, no new RLS policy, no new action, no change to how `/calendar` or the planner consume work schedules — D-068's per-person `show_work_schedule_on_calendar` flag already defaults `true` for `self`, so entries added here appear on the calendar and in planner scoring immediately with zero additional wiring.

**Tests/verification:** `pnpm exec tsc --noEmit`, `pnpm exec eslint .`, `pnpm vitest run` (430 tests, 44 files, all passing), `pnpm run build` all clean — `/settings` still builds as a dynamic route.

**Live-verified (production, second pass, fresh session):** logged into a brand-new browser session (not just client-side revalidation from the build/verification session), loaded `/settings` cold, confirmed "Your work schedule" and "Your time off" cards render cleanly with no lingering test data (the earlier "Mon Work 09:00-17:00" verification entry was fully deleted, list correctly empty). D-092 is fully complete and closed.

## D-093 | 2026-08-31 | Cron routes had no real auth (CRON_SECRET unset) + service-role household-scoping audit

**Problem found (not from the written spec — surfaced by re-reading KNOWN-ISSUES.md's own "found during this pass, not in the brief" notes while looking for more autonomous work):** every `isAuthorized(request)` check across all five cron routes (`gift-scan`, `brief`, `weekend-plan`, `opportunities`, `calendar-sync`) already existed and was correctly written (`if (!secret) return true; return authHeader === "Bearer " + secret`), but `CRON_SECRET` was never actually set in Vercel production — so the check fail-opened and every cron route was, in practice, callable by anyone who knew the URL, no auth at all. This was flagged as an accepted-but-unfixed risk as far back as D-061 and never closed.

**Fix:** generated a random 32-byte secret and added it as `CRON_SECRET` to Vercel production env (no code change needed — the auth check was already correct and waiting for the env var to exist). Vercel's own Cron Jobs feature automatically attaches a matching `Authorization: Bearer <CRON_SECRET>` header to its own scheduled invocations once the env var is present, so all five existing daily/weekly schedules in `vercel.json` keep running unaffected. Redeployed to production so the new env var takes effect.

**Live-verified:** all five cron endpoints now return `401 {"error":"unauthorized"}` on an unauthenticated request (previously they would have executed the job and returned 200). Did not have Vercel re-trigger a real scheduled run to prove the authorized path (would mean waiting for the next natural schedule slot), but the auth-check code itself was already exercised by existing unit tests and is unchanged — only the previously-missing env var was added.

**Service-role household-scoping audit (the other half of the same KNOWN-ISSUES follow-up item, after D-053's cross-household leak):** reviewed every call site of `createSupabaseServiceRoleClient()` in the repo (5 cron routes, 3 standalone job scripts, 2 server-action files, `lib/ai/client.ts`). Every cron route loops `householdsRepo.list(client)` and passes each household's own `household.id` explicitly into its generator (`generateDailyBrief`, `generateWeekendPlan`, `detectOpportunitiesForHousehold`, `syncCalendarFeed`, `scanHouseholdForGiftOccasions`); every on-demand server action (`app/(app)/actions.ts`, `app/(app)/calendar/actions.ts`) resolves `household.id` from `requireHouseholdContext()` (RLS-scoped, authenticated) before ever touching the service-role client. Every generator internally scopes its repo reads/writes with an explicit `.eq("household_id", householdId)` (or an explicit `household_id` field on create). No new cross-household leak found — D-053 remains the only instance found, and it's already fixed. No code changes from this half of the audit; documenting here so "worth a deliberate audit pass" (KNOWN-ISSUES, logged after D-053) doesn't linger as an open item.

**Documentation-only follow-up:** logged two genuine open product questions that came up during this pass's KNOWN-ISSUES review — Q-004 (desktop/tablet layout: keep the narrow mobile-width column, or build real multi-column desktop layout?) and Q-005 (what should the brief's "look-ahead" backlog item actually mean?) — in `QUESTIONS.md`, since both need Richard's decision and neither was formally logged there before.

## D-094 | 2026-08-31 | Gift history entries were add/delete-only, no edit

**Problem found (from `PROGRESS.md`'s own leftover "no edit, add-only" note, reviewed and confirmed while looking for more autonomous work):** interests, gift budgets, and gift-shopping-site bookmarks were already effectively editable — each is stored with a unique constraint (`person_id, interest` / `person_id, occasion_type` / `person_id, url`) and its "add" action upserts, so re-adding the same interest/occasion/site with new details silently updates the existing row rather than erroring or duplicating. Gift history (`gifts` table) was the one record type with no such path at all: `recordGiftAction` always inserted a new row, so a typo'd description, wrong cost, wrong date, wrong occasion, or wrong reaction on a past gift could only be corrected by deleting the whole entry and re-typing it from scratch.

**Fix:** added `updateGiftAction(personId, giftId, ...)`, reusing `giftInsertSchema` (minus `person_id`/`status`, which don't change post-creation) so validation can't drift from the create path. Added an inline "Edit" control next to each gift-history row (`GiftHistoryItem` in `person-forms.tsx`) that swaps the static row for a pre-filled form with the same fields as "Record a gift" and Save changes / Cancel buttons, instead of a separate always-visible edit form with no natural home on the page. Deliberately does not re-run `applyGiftFeedback` on edit — that already nudged the matched interest's strength once at creation time off whatever reaction was recorded then, and re-running it on every later correction (even one unrelated to the reaction field) would double-count that nudge.

**Verified:** `pnpm exec tsc --noEmit`, `pnpm exec eslint .`, `pnpm vitest run` (44 files / 430 tests), `pnpm run build` all clean. Live-verified on production against Melissa Smith's person record: recorded a test gift, clicked Edit, confirmed the form pre-filled with the exact saved values, changed description/cost, saved, confirmed the row updated immediately and again after a full page reload (server-persisted, not just client state), then deleted the test row via the existing Remove/confirm flow. No test data left behind.

## D-095 | 2026-08-31 | Activities could only ever have one location, even though the schema always supported many

**Problem found (backlog #2, from the original spec's own example — `docs/ORIGINAL-BUG-SPEC.md` line 47): "One activity, many locations. Golf is modelled as two separate activities; Shooting is one activity with two locations. Make it consistently the latter."** The `activity_locations` table, its RLS policies, and the repo layer (`activityLocationsRepo`, `listLocationsForActivity`, `listActivitiesWithLocations`) already supported a clean one-to-many design, and the activities list page already rendered every location as a badge (`activity.locations.map(...)`). The gap was entirely in the write path: `POST /api/activities` and `PATCH /api/activities/[id]` both only ever read/wrote a single location (`PATCH` explicitly took `existingLocations[0]`), and `ActivityForm` had flat single-location fields with no way to add a second one. So a household could never actually reach the "one activity, many locations" state the spec called for — a second course/range/spot could only be added by creating a whole separate activity, exactly the anti-pattern being fixed.

**Fix (additive, not a refactor of the existing single-location path):** left the existing "usual location" fields and the POST/PATCH route handlers untouched — they keep managing `locations[0]` exactly as before. Added a new section below the edit form (`ActivityLocationsSection` in `activity-locations-section.tsx`) that lists any *additional* locations (`locations[1:]`) with a Remove button per row (via the existing `ConfirmDeleteButton`) and a small form to add another one (name + optional address, with the same Nominatim geocode-on-save fallback as D-070 when no coordinates are given). Backed by two new server actions in `app/(app)/activities/actions.ts`: `addActivityLocationAction` (validates via the existing `activityLocationInsertSchema`, geocodes, inserts) and `removeActivityLocationAction` (ownership-checks the activity before deleting). No changes needed to the list page — it already renders whatever is in `activity.locations`.

**Verified:** `pnpm exec tsc --noEmit`, `pnpm exec eslint .`, `pnpm vitest run` (44 files / 430 tests), `pnpm run build` all clean.

**Live-verified on production** against the real Golf activity (id `5071ebc2-26fd-44cc-9a9e-a2a6cad9ad1b`), which turned out to already have a second, previously-invisible location row (name literally `"Eugene, Oregon"`, likely a stray geocode-fallback artifact from before this feature existed — flagged for Richard to review/rename or delete, not touched here since it's real household data). Added a test location, confirmed it appeared immediately and survived a full page reload, removed it via the confirm-delete flow, confirmed the removal survived a reload too, and left production with only the original pre-existing location. Along the way, found and fixed a real bug: the add-location form wasn't clearing its fields after a successful add (pending-transition-based reset didn't fire reliably) — switched to the same `justSubmittedRef` + effect-on-state pattern already proven by `AddInterestForm` (D-032), redeployed, and re-verified end-to-end (second round-trip: add → clears immediately → reload → remove → reload confirms clean).

## D-096 | 2026-08-31 | Backlog #3 investigation: ConfirmDeleteButton UX polish — arm-timeout dismiss already fixed, added missing focus trap

**Investigation:** the backlog line asked to polish the delete-confirmation UX (arm-timeout / outside-click dismiss). Checked the current `components/ui/dialog.tsx` / `lib/hooks/use-confirm-delete.ts` (used by every `ConfirmDeleteButton` across activities, gifts, interests, budgets, custody schedule, calendar events/custody/time off, gift sites) and found the two-click "arm" pattern this was asking about had already been fully replaced by a real modal dialog in an earlier decision (P0-1/D-069, well before this session): Escape-key dismiss, backdrop-click-to-cancel, and a `pending` state that disables both buttons mid-request are all already implemented. So the literal ask (arm-timeout, outside-click dismiss) no longer applies — there's no arm timer left in the codebase to fix.

**Real gap found and fixed:** the dialog had no keyboard focus trap — with `Tab`, focus could move off the dialog onto elements behind the backdrop (they're only visually obscured by the overlay, not removed from tab order, so keyboard-only users could tab into content they can't currently see or interact with safely). Added a minimal focus trap to the shared `ConfirmDialog` primitive: `Tab`/`Shift+Tab` now wrap between the dialog's first and last focusable element instead of escaping it. One shared component, so every delete-confirmation dialog in the app got this for free — no per-caller changes needed.

**Verified:** `pnpm exec tsc --noEmit`, `pnpm exec eslint .`, `pnpm vitest run` (44 files / 430 tests), `pnpm run build` all clean.

**Live-verified on production:** opened the delete-confirmation dialog on the Golf activity's "Eugene, Oregon" location (real data, not deleted). Focused the Delete button and simulated a real Tab keypress via CDP-level input — focus correctly wrapped to Cancel instead of escaping the dialog. Confirmed Escape still dismisses the dialog as before. No regression to the existing cancel/confirm/pending behavior.

## D-097 | 2026-08-31 | Backlog #4: individual custody-block edit — direct edit for one-off blocks, exception-routing preserved for schedule-generated ones

**Problem found (backlog #4):** `custody_blocks` is one table used two ways — one-off blocks created directly via the one-off form (`custody_schedule_id` null) and schedule-generated blocks materialized by `materializeCustodySchedule()` in `lib/custody/materialize.ts` (`custody_schedule_id` set). There was no way to edit a single block's fields after creation. A naive "add an Edit button everywhere" fix would have been actively harmful for schedule-generated blocks: `materializeCustodySchedule()` deletes and re-inserts every block in the rolling 90-day window (`MATERIALIZE_WINDOW_DAYS`) whenever the schedule or its exceptions change, so any direct edit to a schedule-generated block's fields would be silently overwritten on the next materialization — a data-loss trap with no visible warning.

**Decision (scoped, not a refactor of materialization):** only build direct-edit UI for one-off blocks. For schedule-generated blocks, keep routing edits through the existing, materialization-safe `custody_schedule_exceptions` mechanism (`ExceptionForm`), which already lets a user override just the responsible person for one date and is respected across re-materialization. This matches the ground rule of making one path the source of truth rather than patching both: one-off blocks are edited directly; schedule-generated blocks are only ever edited via exceptions.

**Implementation:**
- `lib/db/schemas.ts` — added `custodyBlockUpdateSchema` (omits `household_id`/`custody_schedule_id`, same start-before-end `.refine()` as the insert schema).
- `app/api/calendar/custody/[id]/route.ts` (new) — `PATCH` handler: 404s if the block doesn't exist or belongs to another household; **400s with an explanatory message** ("This block comes from a recurring schedule — add an exception on that schedule instead of editing it directly.") if `existing.custody_schedule_id` is set; otherwise validates via `custodyBlockUpdateSchema` and updates.
- `custody-block-form.tsx` generalized to accept `endpoint`/`method`/`defaults`/`redirectTo` props so the same form now serves both create (`POST /api/calendar/custody`) and edit (`PATCH /api/calendar/custody/[id]`).
- `app/(app)/calendar/custody/one-off/[id]/edit/page.tsx` (new) — server component that 404s outright if the block is schedule-generated, so there's no route that can reach the disallowed edit path even before the API guard is hit.
- Calendar day view (`app/(app)/calendar/page.tsx`) now shows the right affordance per block: a direct Edit link for one-off blocks, and a relabeled "Change who has custody this day" link (routing to the schedule's exception form) for schedule-generated ones.
- Schedule exception page/form now accept a `?date=` query param to pre-fill the exception date and show a contextual hint, so following that link from a specific day lands the user on a form already pointed at the right date instead of an empty picker.

**Verified:** `pnpm exec tsc --noEmit`, `pnpm exec eslint .`, `pnpm vitest run` (44 files / 432 tests, +2 new schema tests), `pnpm run build` all clean; build output confirms the new `/api/calendar/custody/[id]` and `/calendar/custody/one-off/[id]/edit` routes.

**Live-verified on production:** created a one-off custody block, confirmed it in the list and day view, opened its edit page (fields correctly pre-filled), changed the location, saved, confirmed the redirect and that the change persisted after a reload. Followed a schedule-generated block's "Change who has custody this day" link with `?date=2026-09-10` and confirmed the exception form's date field was pre-filled with the contextual message shown. Directly tested the guard on a real schedule-generated block (id `b8229eea-7e0b-45d4-a96b-02e4adac7ff5`, part of Cal's Rio Vista custody schedule): the direct-edit page (`/calendar/custody/one-off/[id]/edit`) 404s for it, and a direct `PATCH /api/calendar/custody/[id]` call against it returns `400` with the expected message — confirming both the UI and the API independently refuse to let a schedule-generated block be edited outside the exception mechanism. Cleaned up the one-off test block created for verification via the existing delete-confirmation flow and confirmed it was gone after a reload.

## D-098 | 2026-08-31 | Backlog #5: traced the stray "accepted" invite for invitee3 to a real (now-fixed) display gap, not a live bug or unexplained data corruption

**Context:** Backlog item asked to review a `household_invites` row for `rwsmith964+invitee3@gmail.com` that shows `status=accepted` targeting the real Smith Household (`312f2e2e-63a8-4a61-88d7-4f1c29e23ef8`), even though that account currently has no `household_members` or `people` row in Smith Household at all — its only real membership today is as owner of its own separate "Invitee3 Personal Household" (`76285864-8df1-4ca2-8309-a1e979b798c2`). This directly contradicts D-069's own speculative note on the same finding ("suggesting the accept flow let them land in their own household rather than joining Smith Household") and appears to contradict D-055, which explicitly confirmed at the time that invitee3 "had a genuine `household_members` row (role `adult`) in Smith Household."

**Investigation:** Read `accept_household_invite()` (`supabase/migrations/20260827000001_household_invites.sql`) end-to-end — it is correct: it inserts into `household_members` using `inv.household_id` (the invite's own target, never the caller's own household), and the `status='accepted'` update only commits if that insert didn't throw (PL/pgSQL functions run in an implicit transaction; an uncaught exception rolls back the whole call, including the status write). So the accept RPC could not have produced today's exact state on its own — D-055's contemporaneous confirmation that the Smith Household membership row genuinely existed right after accept is consistent with the code, and is trustworthy.

The real explanation is elsewhere: `removeMemberAction` (owner removes a member) and `leaveHouseholdAction` (a member leaves under their own steam) both legitimately `DELETE` a `household_members` row — and neither one touches the originating `household_invites` row's `status` at all, because the two are only ever linked one direction (accept writes `accepted_by_user_id` onto the invite; nothing writes back the other way). So a member who accepts an invite and later leaves (or is removed) leaves their invite stuck showing bare "Accepted" forever — correctly describing the invite's own history, but misleadingly implying current membership to anyone reading it in Settings. Given D-069's own test-account activity around this same account three days later (Aug 30, using "a real invitee account" for verification, deleting the leaked "Invitee Three" person), the most likely sequence is that invitee3 left or was removed from Smith Household sometime between D-055 and D-069's passes — not a corrupted accept flow, not manual SQL tampering, and not a new bug.

**Decision:** Rather than adding a new persisted invite status (a bigger schema change than this gap warrants, and the invite's status column is correctly a historical record of the invite itself, not a live membership flag), fixed the *display* layer: `app/(app)/settings/household-members.tsx`'s "Invite history" list now cross-checks each accepted invite's `accepted_by_user_id` against the household's current live members (`members`, already loaded on the same Settings page) and renders "Accepted, then left" instead of bare "Accepted" whenever the accepting user is no longer an active member. This self-heals automatically for any future member who leaves after accepting — no per-instance cleanup needed, and no schema or RPC change.

**Two other stray rows found during this investigation, left untouched (informational only):** (1) a pre-existing seed/dummy "Smith Household" (`20000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000001`) with its own placeholder "Richard Smith" person (`user_id=NULL`) and no `household_members` row — orphaned demo-seed data, already referenced elsewhere (D-053's writeup) as the source of the cross-household gift-suggestion leak, unrelated to backlog #5; (2) the "Invitee3 Personal Household" and its owning auth account are themselves leftover test infrastructure from D-055-era invite testing. Deleting any of these is a destructive production write on data that doesn't touch Richard's real family records, so — consistent with this session's standing rule that destructive Supabase deletes need `confirm_action`, and with no one available to confirm overnight — none were deleted. Flagged in the status report as optional cleanup for Richard's discretion, not a functional problem.

**Clarifying D-064, not correcting it:** D-064 (2026-08-29) accurately described the state that existed *at that time* — Smith Household genuinely did have two real `self` people (Richard and "Invitee Three") before D-069's Aug 30 pass deleted the leaked "Invitee Three" person as part of its own Fix 5. D-064's text is a correct historical record of a state that was later intentionally changed by a subsequent decision, not an error needing retroactive correction.

**Verification:** `pnpm typecheck && pnpm lint && pnpm test -- --run` (44 files / 432 tests, unchanged) `&& pnpm build` all passed cleanly. Committed (`474061e`), pushed to `origin/main`, deployed via `vercel deploy --prod`, aliased to `lifeos-seven-rho.vercel.app`, confirmed Ready. Live-verified against production: logged in as Richard, expanded Settings > Household members > Invite history, and confirmed `rwsmith964+invitee3@gmail.com` now reads "Accepted, then left" while the two already-revoked test invites (`invitetest`, `invitee2`) are unaffected and still read "Revoked." Richard's own household roster (himself, Owner) is correct and was never affected by any of this — the mismatch was entirely confined to leftover test-account data, never Richard's real family.

## D-099 | 2026-08-31 | Track B kickoff: Capacitor hosted-app wrapper + App Store launch plan

**Context:** In parallel with desktop-layout mockup work (Track A, on branch `desktop-mockups`, awaiting Richard's review), Richard asked to start prepping the App Store path now rather than wait for the desktop work to finish.

**Decision:** LifeOS is a server-rendered Next.js app (Server Actions, Supabase auth cookies, per-household dynamic data) that cannot be statically exported, so the only viable Capacitor shape is a "hosted" wrapper: the native WebView's `server.url` points directly at the live production deployment (`https://lifeos-seven-rho.vercel.app`) rather than bundling static assets. This means the native shell contributes an app icon, native APIs, and (once built) push notifications/biometrics/widgets — the actual UI is still served fresh by Vercel/Supabase, unchanged.

**What was done:**
- Added `@capacitor/core`, `@capacitor/cli`, `@capacitor/ios`, `@capacitor/android` as devDependencies.
- Created `capacitor.config.ts` — `appId: com.rwsmith.lifeos`, hosted mode with `server.url` set to production.
- Ran `npx cap add ios`, which successfully generated `ios/App/App.xcodeproj` and supporting files (this step does not require Xcode — only *building/archiving* it does).
- Drafted `APP-STORE-PLAN.md`: the Guideline 4.2 "Minimum Functionality" risk a plain webview wrapper carries, three native features that address it (push notifications for custody/gift reminders, Face ID/biometric app-lock, a home-screen "today's brief" widget), the macOS build-path options since Xcode requires macOS and Richard's own device is Windows (GitHub Actions macOS runner recommended first, Codemagic/Capawesome Cloud or a borrowed Mac as alternatives), the $99/year Apple Developer Program enrollment Richard must complete personally, the store-listing assets still needed (1024×1024 icon, 1320×2868 screenshots, hosted privacy-policy and support URLs, demo credentials), and the submission pipeline once a macOS build path exists.

**What could not be done here:** Any actual Xcode build, archive, code-signing, simulator run, or TestFlight upload — this sandbox is Linux-only and Xcode has no Linux equivalent. The generated `ios/` project is real and will be usable once a macOS build path (§5 of the plan) is set up, but nothing beyond generating it was possible in this environment. No native plugin code (push/biometric/widget) has been written yet — that work needs the native build path to exist first so it can actually be tested.

**Verification:** `pnpm exec tsc --noEmit`, `pnpm run lint`, `pnpm test -- --run` (44 files / 432 tests, unchanged), and `pnpm run build` all passed cleanly after these changes — confirms the Capacitor additions are inert from the deployed Next.js app's perspective (devDependencies + a config file + docs; nothing in the web app's runtime path references `capacitor.config.ts`). Merged to `main`, will push and redeploy to production as a routine confirmation that the live site is unaffected; this decision has no user-facing web change to live-verify beyond "site still loads and behaves exactly as before."

**Next steps (Richard's decision needed):** which native feature to build first, whether to enroll in the Apple Developer Program now vs. after native feature code exists, and which macOS build path to set up (§5/§9 of `APP-STORE-PLAN.md`).

## D-100 | 2026-08-31 | Biometric app-lock for the Capacitor shell (first native feature)

**Context:** Continuing Track B (App Store prep, D-099). Richard chose biometric app-lock as the first native feature to build (over push notifications) since it needs no Apple Developer account or APNs setup to build and test, and chose "on every app open" (not just after a period of inactivity) as the trigger behavior.

**Decision:** Added `components/native/app-lock-gate.tsx`, a client component wrapping the authenticated app shell (`app/(app)/layout.tsx`). It checks `Capacitor.isNativePlatform()` — when false (i.e. anyone using the public website in a normal browser), it renders children immediately with no lock screen at all, so this feature has zero effect on the live website. When true (running inside the native Capacitor shell), it shows a lock screen and calls `BiometricAuth.authenticate()` (`@aparajita/capacitor-biometric-auth`, the plugin matching Capacitor 8) on cold launch and again on every `App` `resume` event (`@capacitor/app`), matching "every app open" rather than a single per-install check. A device with no biometrics/passcode enrolled, or a user who cancels the native prompt, unlocks anyway rather than permanently locking someone out of their own family's data — only a genuine authentication failure keeps the lock screen up, with a manual "Unlock" retry button.

**What could not be verified here:** Real Face ID/Touch ID prompts, since this sandbox is Linux-only with no iOS/Android simulator or device — that requires the macOS build path from D-099's plan (§5) to exist first. What *was* verified: the plugin's types match its actual exported API (`BiometryError`, `BiometryErrorType`, `AuthenticateOptions` fields), and — most importantly for a feature that touches every authenticated page — that the public website is completely unaffected: loaded the Brief page in a plain browser against the local dev server and confirmed no lock screen renders (`Capacitor.isNativePlatform()` correctly evaluates false outside the native shell).

**Verification:** `pnpm exec tsc --noEmit`, `pnpm run lint` (required one fix: deferred the cold-launch `authenticate()` call via `setTimeout` to satisfy `react-hooks/set-state-in-effect`, since calling it synchronously in the effect body was flagged), `pnpm test -- --run` (44 files / 432 tests, unchanged), and `pnpm run build` all passed cleanly. Merged to `main`; will push and redeploy to production as a routine confirmation that the live website is unaffected — like D-099, there is no user-facing web behavior to live-verify beyond "the site still loads and behaves exactly as before," since this feature is entirely inert outside the not-yet-built native shell.

**Next steps:** Once a macOS build path exists (D-099 §5) and the `ios/`/`android/` projects can actually be built, run/test this on a simulator or device, add the required `NSFaceIDUsageDescription` Info.plist entry and `USE_BIOMETRIC` Android permission (both documented in the plugin's README, not yet added to the generated native projects here since they can't be edited/verified without Xcode/Android Studio), and decide whether to build push notifications next.

## D-101 | 2026-08-31 | Ship desktop layout: sidebar nav + grid pages, mobile untouched

**Context:** Richard asked for a better desktop layout — the app previously used the same 375px-max-width single-column mobile layout at every screen size, wasting most of a desktop screen. Two mockup rounds were built and shared (Brief/People/Calendar at 1280px), Richard confirmed he could see them, and approved shipping the sidebar+grid treatment as-is with no alternate needed.

**Decision:** Merged the `desktop-mockups` branch (commit 306f9ad) into `main`. At the `lg` breakpoint and above: a persistent left sidebar replaces the mobile top header + bottom tab bar (nav links, household name, notifications bell, sign-out all move into the sidebar); page content gets a `max-w-6xl` centered container with generous padding instead of the mobile `max-w-md` column; the People and Calendar pages use multi-column grids instead of stacked single-column lists. Below `lg`, every class carries an explicit `lg:` prefix or `lg:hidden` — the mobile experience (top header, fixed bottom nav, single column, capture button position) is byte-for-byte the same JSX as before, just conditionally hidden/shown by breakpoint rather than rewritten, so there was no risk of the "preserve mobile exactly" constraint drifting during the change.

**Merge conflict:** This branch was cut before D-100 (biometric app-lock) existed, so both branches touched `app/(app)/layout.tsx` — D-100 wrapped the whole shell in `<AppLockGate>`, this branch restructured the shell's internals into sidebar+content. Resolved by keeping the full sidebar/grid structure from this branch and wrapping the *entire* result (sidebar included) in `AppLockGate`, so the native biometric lock still gates desktop equally with mobile.

**Verification:** `pnpm exec tsc --noEmit`, `pnpm run lint`, `pnpm test -- --run` (432 tests), `pnpm run build` all clean after the merge. Re-screenshotted both a real logged-in session at 1280px (sidebar, grid Brief page) and 375px (unchanged mobile Brief page) against the local dev server post-merge to confirm the resolved conflict didn't regress either breakpoint.

**Not done / explicitly out of scope:** No alternate desktop treatment (e.g. top nav) was built — Richard approved the one mockup directly, so there was nothing to compare it against.

## D-102 | 2026-08-31 | Android platform prep: real icon/splash, signed release build, Play Store plan

**Context:** Continuing the standing App Store/Play Store readiness directive once desktop layout (D-101) shipped. `npx cap add android` had already scaffolded the `android/` Gradle project on branch `android-play-store-prep`, and the Android SDK/JDK toolchain was already working headlessly in this Linux sandbox (unlike iOS, which needs macOS/Xcode). Two problems remained before this was submission-track-worthy: the app still shipped the generic default Capacitor "blue X" icon and white splash screen instead of the real LifeOS brand mark, and `bundleRelease` was producing an *unsigned* AAB — fine for a local build check, useless for an actual Play Store upload.

**Decision:** Generated the real Android icon and splash assets directly from the existing brand source files (`public/icon-512.png`, `public/icon-512-maskable.png` — black circle/background, white triangle), since `@capacitor/assets`' bundled `sharp` binary failed to install in this sandbox (missing prebuilt native binding, no matching npm platform build available). Used Pillow instead to produce every required Android density: legacy + round launcher icons and adaptive-icon foreground layers at mdpi through xxxhdpi, an adaptive-icon background color (`#0A0A0A`, sampled from the brand maskable icon) replacing the default white, and a matching branded splash screen (black background, centered white triangle) at every density/orientation Capacitor ships, replacing the default white/blue-logo splash. Also applied the same real icon to the iOS `AppIcon.appiconset` (single 1024×1024 universal icon, no alpha) so both platforms carry the correct brand mark even though iOS can't be built/tested here.

Generated a real release signing keystore (`lifeos-release.keystore`, PKCS12, RSA 2048, 30-year validity, alias `lifeos-release`) with `keytool`, and wired it into `android/app/build.gradle` via a `signingConfigs.release` block that reads store/key passwords from a local `android/keystore.properties` file — never hardcoded into the Gradle file itself. Both the keystore file and `keystore.properties` are git-ignored (`android/.gitignore` previously had the keystore-exclusion lines *commented out* and didn't ignore `keystore.properties` at all — fixed both, since a leaked signing key is permanent and unrecoverable once pushed to any git history).

Corrected an inaccurate note carried over from D-100/D-099: `USE_BIOMETRIC`/`USE_FINGERPRINT` do not need a manual `AndroidManifest.xml` edit — confirmed via `aapt dump badging` on the debug APK that both are already present, auto-merged from the biometric plugin's own bundled manifest.

**What was verified:**
- `./gradlew assembleDebug` — clean build; `aapt dump badging` confirms the app now points at the real adaptive-icon XML (`mipmap-anydpi-v26/ic_launcher.xml`) rather than a stale reference, and the rendered foreground/background composite visually matches the brand mark (black background, centered white triangle, correctly within the adaptive-icon safe zone).
- `./gradlew bundleRelease` — clean build; crucially, `signReleaseBundle` now actually executes (it did not exist as a meaningful step before), and `jarsigner -verify app-release.aab` returns `jar verified` with the real self-signed certificate (expires 2056-08-23) rather than no signature at all.
- `git status`/`git check-ignore -v` confirms `keystore.properties`, `keystore-secure/lifeos-release.keystore`, and `local.properties` are all correctly excluded before staging — none were added to git.
- Full web-side pipeline re-run despite this being Android-only work: `pnpm exec tsc --noEmit`, `pnpm run lint` (0 errors, pre-existing warning count unchanged), `pnpm test -- --run` (44 files / 432 tests, unchanged), `pnpm run build` — all clean, confirming none of this touched the deployed Next.js app's runtime path.
- Updated `APP-STORE-PLAN.md` with a new Android/Play Store section (§10–§14) mirroring the iOS section: what's done, the keystore hand-off Richard needs to action, the one-time $25 Play Console registration fee (Richard's own action, same rule as Apple's $99/year), and the submission pipeline once Play Console access exists.

**What could not be done here:** Real on-device/emulator testing (no Android emulator display in this sandbox — Gradle CLI builds are headless-verified only), and all remaining Play Console store-listing assets (feature graphic, hi-res icon upload, screenshots, descriptions, content rating, Data Safety form) — those don't require a build environment, just haven't been created yet.

**Next steps:** Share the generated keystore file and its password with Richard directly and flag that he must back it up somewhere durable outside this sandbox (§11 of the plan) before it's needed for a first Play Store upload. Richard needs to register a Google Play Console account ($25 one-time, his own action) before any real upload/testing-track step can happen. Commit this branch's work and merge to `main`.

## D-103 | 2026-08-31 | Public /privacy policy page — required for both store submissions

**Context:** Both App Store Connect and Google Play Console require a live, public privacy-policy URL before a listing can be submitted — a repo-only markdown file (`docs/privacy.md`) doesn't satisfy that, and that existing file is also the wrong document for the purpose: it's an internal engineering note about redacting child data before Anthropic API calls, not a consumer-facing policy covering what's collected, who it's shared with, retention, and contact info.

**Decision:** Added a public `/privacy` route (`app/privacy/page.tsx`, plain server component, no auth required) with a consumer-facing privacy policy reflecting LifeOS's actual data practices: what's collected (account credentials, household/family data including children's schedules and locations, calendar/activity data, AI-feature prompt content), how children's data is specifically handled (parent-entered, not collected from the child directly; names/locations replaced with placeholders before any Anthropic API call, matching the real behavior documented in `docs/privacy.md`/`lib/ai/context.ts`), the four third-party processors data is shared with (Supabase, Anthropic, Resend, Vercel) with no data sale, retention/deletion via a contact email, and a security note on HTTPS + row-level-security household isolation.

**Important — flagged to Richard, not decided unilaterally:** this is a first draft written from the app's actual behavior, not a substitute for Richard's own review. It's a real legal document once submitted to app stores or shown to users, and covers children's data (COPPA-adjacent territory) — Richard should read it and confirm it's accurate and sufficient before it's relied on for an actual submission.

**Verification:** Required two lint fixes (unescaped apostrophes/quotes in JSX text, `react/no-unescaped-entities`). `pnpm exec tsc --noEmit`, `pnpm run lint` (0 errors), `pnpm test -- --run` (432 tests, unchanged), `pnpm run build` all passed. Pushed and deployed to production; live-verified `https://lifeos-seven-rho.vercel.app/privacy` loads publicly (no login prompt) with correct content and title. Also discovered and fixed a deploy-pipeline issue while redeploying D-102: `vercel deploy` was uploading the entire working directory including the new 107MB `android/` project, aborting the upload — added `.vercelignore` excluding `android`/`ios` (native platform projects aren't part of the web deployment) and confirmed the deploy succeeded afterward.
